#2793. 'Genius' batted ball on kick return helps Seahawks burn clock, sparks officiating confusion in win over Raiders

A bizarre play that the CBS broadcast hailed as "genius" and drew a pat on the back from head coach Mike Macdonald helped the Seattle Seahawks run out the clock on their 21-13 win over the Las Vegas Raiders on Sunday.

It also sparked officiating confusion that remained unsettled postgame.

The play in question took place on a Las Vegas kickoff with 2:04 remaining and Seattle holding a 21-13 lead. Matt Gay's kick appeared destined to go out of the end zone for a touchback with no time taken off the clock that would have given Seattle the ball at the 35-yard line.

But Tory Horton had different ideas. The Seahawks return man leapt and batted the ball toward the field of play before it could sail out of bounds. He then instructed Rashid Shaheed to pick it up and return it. Shaheed obliged, and when he was tackled at the 19-yard line, the game clock had run down to 1:59, inside the 2-minute warning.

When the play was done, Macdonald greeted Horton with a smile, a high-five and a pat on the back.

Horton's decision to keep the ball in play cost Seattle 16 yards. More importantly, it eliminated the 2-minute warning from Seattle's ensuing offensive possession. It essentially robbed the Raiders of a valuable de facto timeout.

The Seahawks went on to get a first down and kneel out the clock on the win in victory formation. Even if they hadn't managed a first down, Las Vegas, which had two timeouts remaining, wouldn't have been able to get the ball back with any substantial time on the clock thanks to Horton's play. His decision and Shaheed's execution essentially eliminated 40 seconds of game clock.

After the game, Horton discussed his decision with reporters and what was discussed with coaches prior to to the kick.

"All he said was keep the ball in play, field every return, just don't let it go out," Horton said, apparently referring to special teams coach Jay Harbaugh. "Make sure we don't kneel it in the end zone."

From there, the situational awareness and decision-making was all on Horton.

"I took into consideration, I'm gonna try by any means keep this ball in play so we can get a return started. … I made sure I knew where I was on the field before I leaped up, made sure I was in bounds taking off.

"It's just the situational awareness of like knowing the distance and the time and the situation of the game of knowing, all right, this is a must return. .. I just wanted to make sure I made the smartest play for us."

The legality of the play wasn't immediately clear. Officials conferred at the play's conclusion and held a lengthy discussion before addressing the crowd.

After meeting with the Raiders' sideline, referee Bill Vinovich offered this explanation that didn't exactly provide clarity:

CBS rules analyst Gene Steratore was left perplexed after watching the ordeal and didn't agree that a penalty had actually occurred.

"It sounds to me like Bill is saying that there was maybe a foul," Steratore said. "I don't think it's a foul. It's a kick that never hits the ground. I believe you can bat a kick in the air, provided it has never hit the ground yet."

Per the game's official play-by-play, the play was, indeed, ruled an "illegal bat." And the Raiders declined the penalty, since accepting it wouldn't have put time back on the clock.

Here's the definition of an illegal bat, per section 12-5-1 of the NFL rulebook:

any player bats or punches a loose ball in the field of play toward his opponent's goal line;

any player bats or punches a loose ball (that has touched the ground) in any direction, if it is in either end zone;

an offensive player bats a backward pass in flight toward his opponent's goal line

a forward pass that is controlled by a player prior to completing the catch is thrown forward (the ball remains alive if caught by a teammate or intercepted).

Exception: A forward pass in flight may be tipped, batted, or deflected in any direction by any eligible player at any time.

Is the ball in the "field of play" when it's sailing out of bound without having touched the ground on a kickoff? If so, the bat would appear to be illegal per subsection 1 above. If not, officials got it wrong.

Steratore appeared to have referenced subsection 2, which declares the bat of a ball "in either end zone" illegal when the ball "has touched the ground." The ball hadn't touched the ground, so this would not apply to Horton's play in determining that the bat was illegal.

The rulebook defines the "field of play" as "the area bounded by goal lines and sidelines." It goes on to define the end zones separately as "the areas bounded by goal lines, end lines, and sidelines." This appears to add up to Horton's play having legally taken place outside the "field of play" and officials getting it wrong.

Even if the Raiders had accepted the penalty, time wasn't going to be put back on the clock. The goal of pushing the clock under the 2-minute warning was accomplished, and it turned out to be a heads-up play by Horton that few if anybody watching Sunday had seen before — legal or not.

→ Baca artikel asli

#2792. Always one of a kind, Oubre reflects on Sixers stint, why he chose Pacers

Always one of a kind, Oubre reflects on Sixers stint, why he chose Pacers originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Kelly Oubre Jr. experience switched sides Sunday afternoon at Xfinity Mobile Arena.

Back in Philadelphia with the visiting Pacers, Oubre was his usual self. There's never any risk of mistaking Oubre for somebody else.

"Love him," Pacers head coach Rick Carlisle said pregame. "Such an upbeat guy. He's such a unique person. Love the wardrobe, all the different stuff. He's a very authentically one-of-a-kind type guy."

Carlise then detailed Oubre's various basketball qualities, some of which he showed in Indiana's 131-124 loss. The 30-year-old lefty wing scored 16 points and grabbed four rebounds. His day also included a theatrical imitation of Jaylen Brown's off-arm push after the Sixers' star was whistled for an offensive foul.

Sixers fans grew familiiar with Oubre's strengths, weaknesses and eccentricities during his three seasons as a Sixer. He wound up becoming the team's third-longest tenured player behind only Joel Embiid and Tyrese Maxey. Oubre played 178 regular-season games and averaged 14.9 points, 5.4 rebounds, 1.6 assists and 1.3 steals.

After the Sixers replaced president of basketball operations Daryl Morey with Mike Gansey, Oubre signed a two-year contract with the Pacers in free agency.

"Mike came over from Cleveland and I heard whispers that he wanted some of his Cleveland guys," Oubre said before the game in the visitors' locker room. "And I was like, 'OK, cool. I'm a Philly guy. So I guess when he comes over, I will no longer be a Philly guy.' That was kind of the situation at hand.

"No bad blood because business is business at the end of the day. They have a great team over there, and they put together a team that they think is the best for them. I'll be all right elsewhere. I figured it out."

Oubre thought Indiana made sense as his next stop.

"It was the pace, the culture, the attitude that this team has," he said. "It's a fast-paced style of basketball. It's a bunch of guys who work collectively as a unit and kind of figure it out from there.

"I thought that would be a great opportunity for me to come and and kind of free-flow play, not have to think. I was wrong. Been thinking a lot, but I'm starting to figure it out and starting to get my rhythm here. … It's a great culture. Coach Rick Carlisle is a legend, Hall of Fame coach. And they wanted me. I love people who want me."

As Oubre settled into a new city, the Sixers had a stunning, transformational summer.

Gansey traded for Jaylen Brown, signed LeBron James, and added veteran role players like (former Cavalier) Dean Wade, Anfernee Simons and Kentavious Caldwell-Pope. Oubre followed along.

"It was must-see TV, like (for) everybody else," he said. "As a basketball fan, it was pretty cool to see where I was kind of reinvent and get new life and new energy. So I was happy with that. Would I have loved to be a part of it? Of course.

"But at the end of the day, I'm a grown man and I've got to keep it moving and figure it out for myself. It was entertaining. … I'm kind of happy for all the people that have sacrificed over these years. … I'm trying to put a road block in that road for y'all."

Oubre joined the Sixers on a minimum-salary deal in September of 2023 after averaging 20.3 points the past season with the Hornets. Head coach Nick Nurse pitched Oubre on his potential to be a valuable, multi-dimensional player for good teams.

"I was literally about to be done with basketball," he said. "Coach Nurse called me multiple times and said, 'I want you to play for me.' And I was like, 'You know what? This guy really seems to care. He really wants me.' So thank you for just pulling me out of that pit and that hole that I was in four years ago. I got some new life in my basketball career.

"I'm always thankful for that and I love Nurse for that. Just appreciative for my time here. The battle wounds and scars that I've been able to build up, it made me a better man."

Throughout his Sixers stint, Oubre often spoke of growth, evolution, adaptation and self-discovery.

He talked again Sunday about his desire to "Be water" and how life as a Sixer changed him.

"I grew a lot, man," Oubre said. "Just the mental fortitude of being able to withstand adversity. Being able to wake up and not necessarily know which soliders are going to battle, just having to put on different hats on any given night. At first it was challenging, because I'm thinking about all the logistics and things and like, 'This is not what they asked me to do.' But at the end of the day, I just bought into being somebody that can be like water and grow with the team.

"It's been a blessing to be able to play with (VJ Edgecombe), be able to watch Tyrese (Maxey) ascend. Those are the guys where we were front line a lot. Just going through war with those guys was amazing. It just taught me mental fortitude. If I can make it through this, I can make it through anything."

Oubre felt he developed a meaningful connection with the Philadelphia fan base over his three years.

"When they talk about the City of Brotherly Love, I think they're talking about your soul, not necessarily about what you can offer them and what you do," he said. "Everybody in this city works hard. They wake up, go to work, keep the city going. And us as the prima donna basketball players, the least we can do is go hard and sacrifice for them so that they have something to cheer about when they come home from work.

"I think that was the relationship that we had. I'm forever indebted and forever grateful to the people in the city of Philadelphia."

#2791. How Rockstar's 'GTA VI' release could reshape gaming, streaming and even paid time off

It's been 13 years since the last installment of Grand Theft Auto, and fans are getting ready for what analysts say could be the biggest video game launch of all time.

The latest game in the franchise, "Grand Theft Auto VI," is set to launch on Nov. 19, after Take-Two Interactive's Rockstar Games delayed its launch twice. Roth analyst Eric Handler estimated the game maker spent more than $1 billion developing the game.

Sales figures could end up breaking records. Sensor Tower estimated over 12 million preorders through Nov. 13, and Morgan Stanley forecast the game could sell 40 million copies by March 2027.

Beyond launch week, the Grand Theft Auto, or GTA, franchise has a track record of lasting engagement over many years. Even 13 years after launch, "Grand Theft Auto V" is one of the top five most viewed games on Twitch this year.

In anticipation of the game's launch, investors have been taking note: Take-two's stock has more than doubled since the beginning of 2023.

The release is seen as not only a monumental moment for developer Rockstar Games and parent company Take-Two, but also a massive opportunity for the entire gaming industry.

Take-Two CEO Strauss Zelnick addressed demand in a recent "Squawk Box" interview, saying, "We tend to be pretty judicious when we talk about this sort of thing, but our expectations were high and they are being vastly exceeded so far."

A creator who goes by the name Deansocool, who has 375,000 followers on Twitch and posts content to YouTube and other social platforms as well, said "GTA VI" will enable creators to grow their audience and provide a massive opportunity for newer streamers to make a name for themselves.

"This game is going to make a lot of millionaires in 2027. A lot of creators gotta understand this is it. This is that golden ticket. This is like a hundred times bigger than Fortnite," he said in an interview with CNBC.

"It goes beyond this launch moment into this sustaining opportunity for them to grow, build, engage and retain their audience and ultimately support themselves by making money," said Twitch Chief Product Officer Mike Minton.

The release of the latest Grand Theft Auto title could draw so much attention that its impact will be felt beyond just the world of gaming.

BambooHR, a workplace time-off platform, reported that paid time off requests for the "GTA VI" release date are nearly 13% higher than equivalent days in 2024 and 2025. It also noted that nearly one in five workers are planning to take time off for the launch.

PwC issued a report examining all the different sectors of the economy that could benefit from the game's launch — or be hurt by it. The firm projected that food delivery companies could see a surge from hunkered-down gamers, and Miami tourism could benefit. PwC noted that foreign visitors to Seoul's Bukchon Hanok Village, which inspired a location in the hit Netflix movie KPop Demon Hunters, increased 118% in the four weeks following the movie's debut.

Music streaming also looks set to get a boost. Tom Petty's "Love Is a Long Road," which appeared in the first "GTA VI" trailer in December 2023, reportedly saw Spotify streams jump by more than 37,000%.

On the downside, the game could distract from movies and streaming entertainment.

"How are people spending Thanksgiving? There's a lot of sports on TV, but you've also got a pretty solid group of your demographics that are going to be focused on playing 'GTA VI,'" said Bart Spiegel, who leads PwC's Global Entertainment & Media sector. "You're going to start to pull eyeballs away from video streaming platforms.

"I think you're going to hear a lot of analyst calls in the first quarter of 2027 saying, 'Hey, listen, some of our numbers were down because of the release of this game,'" Spiegel added.

Rockstar Games declined CNBC's interview request. Watch the video above to learn more.

#2790. Is a bottom in for the Nasdaq-100? Charts to watch

(See the video above for Frank's complete chart breakdown.)

Is the Nasdaq-100's recent bounce a sign that the bottom is in? We're going to look at several charts to help answer that question.

First, let's remember how we got here. In September, the NDX broke out from a bullish cup-and-handle pattern, producing a 33,200-upside target. That breakout quickly failed, however, and gave way to a bearish pattern that broke down aggressively two weeks ago.

The decline brought the index relatively close to its 26,350 downside target before the current rebound began.

Zooming out, it's telling to see where the bounce originated. Three important forms of support converged in the 25,000-26,000 area:

This confluence of support helped encourage buying after a roughly 15% decline.

For perspective, we also see what larger drawdowns of 20%, 30%, 40%, and 50% would look like … a lot worse.

The following chart tracks NDX drawdowns from 52-week highs going back to just after the financial crisis. Only a handful of declines exceeded 20%, and three produced relatively quick reversals: late 2018, the Covid lows in 2020 and April 2025.

Historically, buying into declines of approximately 15%–25% has generally paid off, even when additional downside occurred first (yellow highlights).

The chart also highlights previous declines of 10%–16% in green, putting the current pullback into perspective.

The major exception was 2022, when the NDX eventually declined nearly 36% over approximately 10 months ... a much worse outcome, which many investors are concerned could happen again now.

That raises the key question: Are we experiencing another routine correction, or the early stages of a larger bear market?

One important clue will be how far this bounce progresses.

Any rally that fails to reclaim the previous highs could establish a significant lower high. Additional lower highs would provide stronger evidence of a developing, longer downtrend.

We saw this in March 2022, when that first sharp rebound effort failed to reach the previous high before a much more severe decline followed.

The bottom line: The current bounce is encouraging, particularly given where it originated and knowing how well buying prior 10-20% drawdowns has paid off.

From here, it's all about the follow through.  We don't need to see new highs right away, but any rally from here that's met with another wave of strong selling could reignite downside momentum.

Frank Cappelleri is the founder and president of CappThesis, an independent research firm that helps active investors through time-tested chart and statistical analysis. Prior to starting CappThesis, he spent 25 years on Wall Street as an equity sales trader, technical analyst, research sales specialist and desk strategist.

#2788. 32 things we learned in NFL Week 10: Trade deadline already delivering big benefits

The 32 things we learned from Week 10 of the 2026 NFL season:

0. The still-winless Los Angeles Chargers remain on pace to become the NFL's first-ever 0-17 team … unless they can notch their first victory of 2026 in prime time on Monday night in Baltimore, when Jacoby Brissett is set to make his debut as the Ravens' latest QB1.

1. The number of games every NFL team – save the Chicago Bears, Denver Broncos, Philadelphia Eagles and Tampa Bay Buccaneers, who were all on their bye week – has played since the Nov. 10 trade deadline. And those that delved into the market experienced almost immediate returns.

2. Once and present and future Minnesota Vikings QB Kirk Cousins only needed five days to get his former and current team back on track – leading the Vikes to a resounding 30-10 victory over the Green Bay Packers just six days after they were embarrassed by the Buffalo Bills on the "Monday Night Football" prime-time platform.

2a. And it was a meaningful win for an emotional Cousins, whose previous game with the Vikings had also been at Lambeau Field – one during which he tore his Achilles tendon in a game midway through a 2023 season that was shaping up as one of the best years of his career up to that point. Minnesota did not re-sign Cousins in 2024, and he joined the Atlanta Falcons.

3. New Kansas City Chiefs WR Josh Downs seemed to fit into his new circumstances snugly, catching four balls for 53 yards and a TD in his K.C. debut. Downs also assumed the punt return duties for his new team.

4. The number of NFL officials who worked a pair of games Sunday in the aftermath of the FAA's Friday ground stop, which caused travel havoc that rippled through the weekend. Referee Don Willard, umpire Terry Killens, field judge Jabir Walker and side judge Justin Larrew worked the Dolphins-Colts matchup at 1 p.m. ET in Indianapolis, then drove to Cincinnati for the "Sunday Night Football" showdown between the Steelers and Bengals. Willard, normally a side judge, not only was on double duty but in an unfamiliar role to boot.

NFL PLAYOFF PICTURE: Two new teams take over first place in AFC; NFC has new No. 2 seed

4a. The number of TD passes thrown Sunday in Cincinnati by Bengals QB Joe Burrow – the 13th time he's reached that threshold in his career. It's also the fifth consecutive game Burrow has accounted for at least four TDs in a game, clearly putting him at the forefront of the MVP conversation.

5. The number of TD passes thrown Sunday in Atlanta by Chiefs QB Patrick Mahomes – the ninth time, playoffs included, he's reached that threshold in his career but first since Week 1 of the 2022 season.

5a. The number of TD passes thrown Sunday by Dallas Cowboys QB Dak Prescott – the second time, playoffs included, he's reached that threshold in his career … though with far less impact on this day than Mahomes. More on Dallas later.

6. The number of game-winning drives Cleveland Browns QB Deshaun Watson has engineered this season for the 6-4 Browns – i.e., he's done so in all of their games, including Sunday's 27-24 defeat of the Houston Texans, Watson's original club. Cleveland trailed 17-0 early in the second quarter.

6a. Also the number of NFL games the league staged in Europe this season, Sunday's matchup between the Detroit Lions and New England Patriots in Munich the final installment of that sextet.

7. Lions WR Amon-Ra St. Brown, whose mother is German, shone in the contest – which wasn't quite a homecoming for a man who has Deutschland citizenship, but close. He had his first multi-TD game since Week 2 in Detroit's 35-10 blowout victory. "Being in Germany, number one, was a blessing, it's been amazing," said St. Brown afterward, who answered many questions in recent days in German. "But being able to win like that − with the guys, my teammates in front of the fans here in Germany, it's like a dream come true."

8. The number of times Packers QB Jordan Love was sacked Sunday by the blitz-happy Vikings defense. Prior to that nightmare, Love had never been bagged more than five times in an NFL game.

9. Pretty cool that Jacksonville Jaguars head coach gave his new acquisitions, DT Jordan Davis and S Jessie Bates III, Sunday off given neither has had a bye week nor was in line for one given the Jags' remaining schedule.

10. And Jacksonville didn't need its new guys, rolling the Titans 40-23 – the Jaguars' fifth win this season by at least 17 points.

11. We'll know in two months whether or not the Jags overpaid for Bates and Davis, who will cost the franchise one first- and two second-round picks. But the 2026 squad's trajectory was, and continues to be, impressive.

12. While the Cousins deal worked as intended – so far – for Minnesota, Las Vegas Raiders rookie QB Fernando Mendoza maybe wasn't as ready to start as reports coming out of the desert suggested. He had some promising early moments Sunday against the Seattle Seahawks' "Dark Side" defense, but the Silver and Black wound up with fewer than 100 net passing yards in the first regular-season start for the No. 1 pick of the 2026 draft – Mendoza sacked six times for 45 yards in losses … and he didn't seem to see some of the pressure coming at him.

13. The number of penalties committed by the Texans on Sunday, costing them 112 yards. Bad teams, talented or not, find ways to beat themselves.

14. The jersey number of Seattle QB Sam Darnold, who's missed the past two games with various injuries. Sunday, backup QB Drew Lock improved to 3-0 this season and also shepherded the team to victory in Week 1, when Darnold was also hurt. Lock's wins have come against the Arizona Cardinals (twice) and Las Vegas Raiders, but you have to wonder if, at some point, the Seahawks opt for the hot hand. With their bye upcoming, they have another week to decide as Darnold continues to heal.

15. The jersey number of Seahawks WR-KR Tory Horton, who made one of the more heads-up special teams plays you'll ever see in a bid to get his team past the two-minute warning after the Raiders had pulled within one score but had to kick off with 2:04 left on the clock. He leapt high to swat the incoming kickoff forward and then directed teammate Rashid Shaheed to return it and get Seattle inside of the two-minute mark. Horton was flagged for illegally batting the ball on the very unique sequence, but Las Vegas declined it. Regardless, his play achieved its objective, and Seattle prevailed 21-13.

16. Lock completed 14 of 17 passes in Sin City for 240 yards and two TDs, giving him a nearly perfect 158.0 passer rating. However, he did fumble twice, turnovers the same bugaboo that has plagued Darnold.

17. The number of penalties committed by the Carolina Panthers on Sunday, costing them 152 yards – both franchise records. Bad teams, talented or not, find ways to beat themselves – though not necessarily in the abhorrent NFC South, Carolina surviving the New Orleans Saints 20-17 on the road.

18. Thankfully for the Panthers, they still have RB Chuba Hubbard, one of the league's more underrated backs. His 172 yards from scrimmage Sunday represented 61% of Carolina's offensive output. His two TDs similarly provided 60% of his team's points.

19. The Buffalo Bills got seven tackles and a pass defended by new S Amani Hooker, a player the Tennessee Titans were willing to part with.

20. And though you hate to glean too much from wins over the New York Jets (on Sunday), Vikings and Ravens – all teams the Bills have caught without their QB1s for at least a portion of those matchups – Buffalo has allowed just 28 points since its Week 7 bye. The AFC East leaders, which surrendered at least 29 points in five of their first six games this season, have permitted just one touchdown – total – in its past three games.

21. We'll continue to wonder why the Indianapolis Colts didn't trade RB Jonathan Taylor – though, apparently, they didn't get the Godfather offer they wanted, Round 2 draft pick compensation evidently insufficient.

22. Yet the Colts, who are clearly headed into rebuild with their soon-to-be 28-year-old bell cow under contract through the 2028 season, continue to seemingly manage his touches. Taylor had 18 in Sunday's 24-22 defeat of the Miami Dolphins, his third straight game with fewer than 20. (He had at least 20 in six of Indy's first seven game this season.) Maybe Indy is hoping its next GM can trade a fresh Taylor for more draft capital in a few months.

23. We'll also continue to wonder why the Titans didn't trade RB Tony Pollard, who scored his sixth TD in the past three games Sunday but likely only has eight games left with one-win Tennessee before he leaves in free agency. We'll see if the 2028 compensatory pick the franchise might get will be worth whatever it could have reaped for the versatile back, who will turn 30 in the offseason.

24. Los Angeles Rams legend Aaron Donald began Sunday still seeking his first post-unretirement sack. But he got Arizona Cardinals QB Carson Beck 1½ times in the rookie's first regular-season start.

25. And the injury-depleted Rams won their fourth in a row and have rushed for at least 120 yards in each of those victories – including a season-high 202 on Sunday.

26. The San Francisco 49ers' 46-40 defeat of Dallas was more convincing than the final score indicates.

27. The Niners' 584 yards were the most ever piled up against the Cowboys in their own building and, per Elias, San Francisco's three TDs from 60+ yards were tied for the most in a game by one team over the past half-century.

28. Christian McCaffrey's 66- and 74-yard TD catches – mostly runs after the catch – were the two longest of his 10-year career. They also gave him the league's career record for most TD receptions by a running back.

29. And San Francisco, which had already reacquired DT DeForest Buckner, got a nice initial ROI from its latest trade deadline acquisition – WR Keenan Allen catching five balls for 61 yards in his 49ers debut after coming over from the Colts just days before.

30. Is monochrome black slimming? Maybe in the win column. Just ask the Saints.

31. Is monochrome black slimming? Maybe in the win column. Just ask the Jets.

32. Not much to like about the Cowboys this season. But we appreciate them honoring veterans and Medal of Honor winners annually at this time of year by adding a red stripe to the centerline of their helmets as part of the NFL's "Salute to Service" program.

All the NFL news on and off the field. Sign up for USA TODAY's 4th and Monday newsletter.

#2787. Observations after Sixers shake off slow start, Embiid injury scare to win

Observations after Sixers shake off slow start, Embiid injury scare to win originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers brushed off their blowout loss to the Celtics and got back in the win column Thursday night.

They overcame a first-quarter deficit as high as 17 points and earned a 123-112 home win over the Raptors. The Sixers moved to 10-3 and Toronto fell to 5-7.

Tyrese Maxey had 27 points and eight assists. Joel Embiid added 20 points.

Jaylen Brown tallied 18 points and six rebounds. VJ Edgecombe posted 15 points, eight rebounds and a season-high eight assists. Anfernee Simons scored 17 points and shot 5 for 6 from three-point range.

RJ Barrett recorded 31 points and 14 rebounds. Immanuel Quickley had 26 points and six assists.

The Sixers were without LeBron James (low back/sciatica management), Adem Bona (cervical spine sprain), Dominick Barlow (lower back stress reaction) and Ariel Hukporti (right Achilles tendon tear).

The team told reporters Thursday morning that Bona will be re-evaluated in approximately one week and that Barlow participated in shootaround. The 23-year-old forward also went through a pregame workout on the Xfinity Mobile Arena floor. There's no formal timetable for his return.

Toronto's sidelined players included Kawhi Leonard and Scottie Barnes.

The Sixers will face the Pacers on Sunday afternoon in Philadelphia. Here are observations on their victory over the Raptors:

Dean Wade began the game in James' normal spot. The Sixers started slowly, missing their first foul field goals and opening 1 for 8 from three-point range.

He stayed down after setting a ball screen and having an apparent knee-to-knee collision with Ja'Kobe Walter. The Sixers called timeout with 8:30 left in the first quarter and Embiid grabbed at his right knee. He eventually rose with assistance from teammates and Sixers head athletic trainer Kevin Johnson, then limped back to the locker room. Jabari Walker entered.

At the 5:26 mark of the first quarter, Embiid checked back in to cheers from the home fans.

"I knew that he didn't twist it or anything," Maxey said. "I knew it was a knee-to-knee, so I wasn't too worried. He kept saying, 'I just got kneed.' That still hurts, though."

Embiid ended up logging 28 minutes, though he certainly wanted to play more.

"He was very, very motivated to play," Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said. "It was hard to get him out for his scheduled rests tonight, which is a good problem sometimes."

While Embiid's return was a major positive development, the Sixers fell further behind. The Raptors' lead swelled to 32-16 with a Jamison Battle three-pointer and Nurse burned a timeout.

Simons sunk a buzzer-beating three to conclude the first quarter, cutting the Sixers' deficit to nine points.

"Those are big momentum plays that usually have some weight to them and carry you into the next moment," Nurse said.

The Sixers kept rolling against a shorthanded Toronto team playing on the second night of a back-to-back. Simons nailed a triple to kick off the scoring in the second quarter. Brown buried a mid-range jumper and Edgecombe jammed in a crowd-pleasing dunk to give the Sixers a 35-34 edge.

Brown and Edgecombe shined in the second quarter both as individuals and as a duo. The Sixers need Edgecombe to attack a bit more offensively when he shares the floor with Brown and bench players. It doesn't hurt for the second-year guard to have that clarity about his role in those units.

"He's one of our better playmakers," Maxey said of Edgecombe. "He's really good at getting downhill and creating for himself and others."

The Sixers' only three bench rotation players were Walker, Simons and Kentavious Caldwell-Pope (11 points, five assists). In addition to Walker, Wade got center minutes behind Embiid.

Embiid picked up his third foul with 2:23 left in the second quarter and had to take a seat. The teams were tied at 60 apiece through two periods.

The game remained even for much of the third, but Maxey fueled an 11-0 Sixers run late in the quarter.

His speed and the Sixers' collective focus on pushing the pace whenever possible allowed the team to get regular points before Toronto could set its defense. Maxey zoomed in for a layup, then tossed the ball ahead to Brown for an easy bucket. Brown assisted a Simons three. Walker converted a fast-break lay-in and canned a three on the first possession of the fourth quarter. All of a sudden, the Sixers held a 101-87 lead.

Brown, Edgecombe and the bench gave the Sixers more good minutes early in the fourth quarter. They sealed the deal without any late drama. And the fans chanting for a Tacko Fall appearance got their wish with a final-minute entrance by the 7-foot-6 center.

#2785. 'We slept here': Flight delays linger but schedule is back on track for Monday

U.S. flights are set to run close to normal on Monday as airlines scramble to get back on track after the Federal Aviation Administration grounded departures nationwide for more than four hours on Friday.

Disruptions lingered on Sunday with thousands more flights canceled and delayed.

Close to 2,000 U.S. flights were canceled as of 4:45 p.m. ET and more than 7,500 were delayed, according to flight-tracking site FlightAware, down from the 6,697 cancellations and 9,000 delays on Saturday, and Friday's 9,201 cancellations and more than 7,400 delays.

American Airlines had the most cancellations on Sunday at 805 flights, about 13% of its schedule, followed by Delta Air Lines with 360 cancellations, or 7% of its schedule, according to aviation data firm Cirium. The airlines' home hubs of Dallas-Fort Worth International Airport and Atlanta Hartsfield-Jackson International Airport, respectively, saw some of the most severe delays in the U.S. Meanwhile, JetBlue Airways canceled close to a third of its flights.

"We slept here," said Beth Martin, who was trying to travel home to Birmingham, Alabama, from New York's LaGuardia Airport on Sunday at the end of her daughter's birthday trip. They were booked for Saturday night, and when delay announcements started rolling in from Delta Air Lines, they decided to make good use of the extra time: after strolling through Central Park, they went window shopping on Fifth Avenue.

But when the flight was canceled, the standby list for a backup flight looked grim, and their original Midtown hotel was charging about $700 for an additional night, the two slept at LaGuardia on Saturday. She said they found wheelchairs to sleep in outside security.

Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy on Saturday tied the issue to a router and work to update from copper wires, a system overseen by L3Harris Technologies, to a fiber-optic system. FAA administrator Bryan Bedford said the upgrades were part of "routine" maintenance to switch over to fiber optic.

"We are supporting the FAA in its investigation to understand the conditions that led to the Nov. 13 ground stop of our nation's air travel," L3Harris said in a statement Saturday afternoon.

Airlines waived change fees and fare differences and offered pilots and flight attendants extra pay to ease the delays.

Customers told CNBC they were booked on several flights before getting on board.

Justine Fritz, who works in sales for a consumer goods company, was in New York for a business event on Friday. She tried five different flights before getting on a flight to Houston, then flew four hours out of her way to get back to Minneapolis after her Saturday flight was canceled. Fritz said she had so many boarding passes that TSA wouldn't let her through security at first.

"It's been bananas," she told CNBC at the gate. Fritz said she made the best of it by shopping and getting a haircut, but she's still disappointed.

"I'm missing the first hour of the Vikings game," she said.

Martin said her trip home with her daughter will take even longer. Delta booked them to Memphis, and her husband will drive the four hours from Birmingham to pick them up.

Shortly after the groundstop, the longest since 9/11, Duffy pushed Congress for as much as $50 billion for upgrades to the aging U.S. air traffic control system. A day later, Democratic lawmakers pushed back, citing unspent money from existing funding, which includes Congress's approval last year of $12.5 billion for airport and air traffic control improvements over five years.

"Our committee, on behalf of the flying public, will hold the administration accountable for what happened to make sure this chaos does not happen again," said Reps. Rick Larsen (D.-Wash.), ranking member of the House Committee on Transportation and Infrastructure, and Rep. Andre Carson (D.-Ind.), ranking member of the aviation subcommittee, said in a joint statement.

#2783. Japan's economy unexpectedly contracts in third-quarter as elevated energy costs bite

Japan's economy unexpectedly contracted in the third quarter, shrinking at an annualized 0.1% compared to the previous quarter, as elevated energy prices weighed on consumption and net exports.

A Reuters poll of economists had estimated the economy to grow at 0.3% in the three months to September, while GDP had expanded at 1.4% in the previous quarter.

Japan's exports beat expectations for two of the three months in the quarter, but net exports weighed on the economy due to higher costs of energy imports, cutting 0.6 percentage point from the country's growth.

Energy subsidies for households had reportedly expired in September, exposing consumers to higher energy prices brought about by the Iran war.

The GDP data could complicate Japan's policy normalization path, though there's still a 75.6% chance of the Bank of Japan raising rates by 25 basis points in December, according to LSEG.

"Today's GDP data is hardly a green light for the BOJ to hike in December," according to Krishna Bhimavarapu, APAC economist at State Street Investment Management.

He pointed out that real final sales of domestic product fell 2.1% annualized, which is the largest decline outside the Covid-19 pandemic and the first quarter of 2022. "[This] looks to be a loss of momentum," he added. That metric is a measure of

Real final sales of domestic product refer to GDP growth, net of private inventories, and indicate underlying demand.

Marcel Thieliant, head of Asia Pacific at Capital Economics, still expects the BOJ to go ahead with the rate hike. "It looks increasingly likely that the Bank will have to tighten policy more aggressively than we've been anticipating as inflation is about to surge." The BOJ had declared that its 2% underlying inflation target had been met for the first time in 13 years at its meeting last month. Core inflation in September also hit its highest level this year at 2.1%, overshooting analysts' expectations.

On an year-on-year basis Japan's economy grew 1.1%, its strongest growth since the second quarter of 2025. Theliant noted that this was owed to a lower base in the third quarter of 2025.

Following the data release, the Nikkei 225 fell 0.62%, while the yield on the benchmark 10-year Japanese Government Bonds was at 2.885%. The yen weakened marginally against the dollar, trading at 159.76.

#2781. Bengals Dismantle Steelers, Take Sole Possession of First Place in AFC North

The Cincinnati Bengals dismantled the Pittsburgh Steelers 38-18 on Sunday Night Football at Paycor Stadium, taking sole possession of first place in the AFC North. The victory avenged a 30-27 loss in Week 3, extended Cincinnati's winning streak to five games and gave Joe Burrow his first regular-season primetime home win over a division rival.

Cincinnati controlled the game from the opening drive. Evan McPherson opened the scoring with a 24-yard field goal, and Burrow threw touchdown passes to Drew Sample, Chase Brown and Dohnte Meyers for his first career touchdown grab in the second quarter to build a 24-3 halftime lead.

Brown added a second touchdown catch in the third quarter, and Samaje Perine ran one in from a yard out in the fourth. Pittsburgh's only touchdowns came after Cincinnati led 31-3.

The quarterback matchup was completely lopsided. Burrow completed 35 of 44 passes for 409 yards and four touchdowns without an interception, finishing with a 135.7 passer rating. He was sacked only once, and T.J. Watt finished with one tackle and no sacks for the second straight meeting.

Aaron Rodgers completed 15 of 29 passes for 143 yards and two touchdowns, with most of his production coming after the game was decided.

Ja'Marr Chase was the centerpiece of the offense, catching 11 passes for 231 yards on 14 targets. Brown contributed in both phases, rushing 10 times for 44 yards and catching eight passes for 39 yards and two touchdowns. Tee Higgins added five catches for 66 yards.

The Bengals finished with 484 total yards, while Pittsburgh managed only 222.

That gap was a credit to a Cincinnati defense that kept the Steelers off balance all night. Demetrius Knight Jr. led the team with eight tackles, along with a pass breakup and a quarterback hit. Barrett Carter added six tackles and a pass breakup, and Bryan Cook finished with five tackles.

Boye Mafe, Jonathan Allen and Myles Murphy each recorded a sack, and Mafe added three quarterback hits. However, the most impressive part of the performance was how difficult the defense made life for a future Hall of Famer. Rodgers was sacked three times, and Pittsburgh did not reach the end zone until the fourth quarter. Jaylen Warren, who ran for 127 yards in Week 3, was held to 15 yards on seven carries.

The win was a strong example of complementary football. The offense sustained long drives and scored on five of its six red-zone trips, and the defense consistently got the ball back. If the Bengals keep playing at this level, they will be difficult to catch in the division.

Now, the Joe Burrow MVP campaign continues next Monday night, when Cincinnati visits the Washington Commanders in the nation's capital.

#2779. Trump breaks silence about FAA nationwide ground stop, blames Biden administration

President Donald Trump on Sunday made his first public comments about Friday's nationwide ground stop by the Federal Aviation Administration, blaming the Biden administration for making an "old and broken" air traffic control system worse. Trump also urged Congress to approve more funding to modernize the system.

The ground stop lasted more than four hours Friday, disrupted flights nationwide, contributed to thousands of cancellations and delays, and affected hundreds of thousands of passengers.

"The Biden Administration was an FAA disaster," Trump wrote on his social media platform. "Big money spent unwisely, making an old and broken system worse than when they began."

The president added that his administration has "the knowledge and plan for the Flight Control Systems," but said that "we need Congress to give the money, FAST!"

Congress approved $12.5 billion for the FAA last year as part of a major tax and spending package. Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy has said that the aging air traffic control system will need more funding for a broader overhaul.

The FAA said its equipment became unstable after a high volume of data flooded its network. The agency said contractor work at one site may be related and that the underlying cause remains under investigation.

Nationwide FAA ground stops are rare. Before the Nov. 2026 shutdown, the last two were after the Sept. 11 attacks and a brief FAA computer outage in January 2023.

#2777. ‘Hard to win': Simons, Walker carry Portland lessons into Sixers roles

'Hard to win': Simons, Walker carry Portland lessons into Sixers roles originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Anfernee Simons and Jabari Walker shared the floor for 128 games in Portland. The Trail Blazers won 43 of them.

They sat beside one another in the Sixers' interview room Thursday night after a victory and discussed what the other 85 taught them.

"Whether you've got a big role or a small role, it's hard to win in the NBA," Simons said. "We've seen it firsthand."

Their roles were considerably different then.

Simons was an established starter entrusted with creating and scoring. Walker, a 2022 second-round pick, was trying to establish himself in a rotation. During their three overlapping seasons, Portland went 33-49, 21-61 and 36-46. The Blazers missed the playoffs each year.

Both are now reserves on a Sixers team constructed to contend. In nine games they've both appeared in this season, Philadelphia is 7-2.

They provided 26 points, 11 rebounds and six three-pointers in the Sixers' 123-112 win over the Raptors at Xfinity Mobile Arena. Simons scored 17 points and shot 5 for 6 from three-point range. Walker recorded nine points and eight rebounds, including four on the offensive glass.

The Sixers outscored Toronto by 24 points during Simons' 23 minutes. Neither former Blazer committed a turnover.

Their production helped Philadelphia overcome a 17-point first-quarter deficit and improve to 10-3 without LeBron James.

"You learn once you come off the bench to kind of read the game before you get in there," Simons said. "Obviously, we started off kind of slow. You just want to go in, bring some energy, get some stops, get out and run. I think everybody who got off the bench brought energy."

Simons supplied an immediate lift with a deep three-pointer at the first-quarter buzzer. His shot ended an 8-0 Sixers run and trimmed Toronto's lead to nine.

He remained involved during an 11-0 burst late in the third quarter, knocking down a three and setting up Walker for a basket. Walker then made a three early in the fourth before Simons scored on the Sixers' next possession.

When Toronto pulled within six with less than five minutes remaining, Simons answered with his fifth three-pointer.

Making those shots is the most obvious way Simons can help a roster already featuring James, Joel Embiid, Tyrese Maxey and Jaylen Brown. He said the Sixers have not made him feel as though every opportunity must go in.

"They encourage us to keep shooting the ball," Simons said. "Knowing that a team has that much confidence in us to keep shooting makes the game that much easier. Whether you miss or make, as long as we get the right shots in the right moments, I think everybody can live with the misses and makes."

Simons spent seven seasons in Portland before playing last year with Boston and Chicago. His move to Philadelphia reunited him with Walker and Rayan Rupert, another former Blazer who joined the Sixers on a two-way contract during the offseason.

Knowing familiar faces awaited him helped Simons enter another new locker room.

"It made the transition seamless," he said. "Just being able to have some comfortability with guys that I played with for multiple years. It's been fun, kind of feeling like a little bit of old times. Me, Bari and Rups have been through a lot."

Walker's path to Philadelphia began a year earlier and required an additional step.

After playing 188 games over three seasons with Portland, Walker signed a two-way contract with the Sixers in July 2025. He earned a standard contract in February and has since pushed his way into the backup-center conversation despite standing 6-foot-7.

Walker believes some of the work he did on losing teams was easily missed.

"When I first got to the league, I was part of a rebuilding process," he said. "I just felt like some of the little things I did didn't always get noticed when you're losing. I told myself, if I ever got to be part of a winning organization, then I'd go out and expand my abilities."

Rebounding has made Walker difficult for Nick Nurse to overlook.

Walker credited his timing and strong hands to lessons passed down by his father, former NBA center Samaki Walker. Those instincts have become especially useful while the Sixers piece together their frontcourt behind Embiid.

"Rebounding is a skill I kind of honed in on," Walker said. "My dad was a good rebounder. He was known for his rebounding and his timing. I think it's just a gift that he transferred to me."

Walker has made it clear that he expected to earn a role this season.

"I don't know if I was delusional, but I always felt like I was going to play and I was going to be a part," he said. "I read something where they said it's a surprise, but if you were around me this summer, it wouldn't be a surprise. This is expected, and I just want to continue to get better."

The freedom Simons once had in Portland is no longer necessary. Walker does not need a high volume of touches, either. Both understand that playing beside the Sixers' stars requires shooting when open, moving the ball and handling the less glamorous work.

"You've got to find ways to impact the game multiple ways, even if you're not scoring," Simons said. "Whether it's cutting, making the right read and pass to Joel, being the right swing pass to VJ, boxing out on defense or just being in the right position."

Their opportunities will fluctuate. Simons may take nine shots one night and only a few the next. Walker's minutes will depend on matchups and the health of Philadelphia's frontcourt.

Winning has changed what they are being asked to do. Their three years together in Portland taught them why the adjustment is worthwhile.

"You feel appreciative of the times where you go into a situation where your role might be a little bit less, but you're winning," Simons said. "That's something you can't take for granted."

#2776. China’s slowdown deepens in October as industrial output, retail sales, investment miss estimates

China's economic slowdown worsened in October with retail sales, investment and industrial output all missing economists' estimates, as analysts warn a growing reliance on external demand leaves growth more exposed to shocks abroad.

Monday's data showed the investment slump deepened and retail sales again lagged industrial output, as consumer spending languished with the property market downturn eroding household wealth for a fifth year.

Retail sales, a gauge of consumption, rose 0.4% from a year ago in October, disappointing analysts' forecast for a 0.8% rise, slowing from a six-month high of 1.1% in September.

Industrial output expanded 4.8% from a year earlier last month, missing analysts' estimates for 5.3% growth and sharply slowing from 5.5% in September, the highest level in five months.

For the first ten months of this year, fixed-asset investments, including property and infrastructure, shrank 8.1%, deepening a collapse plaguing the economy this year that's unprecedented outside the Covid-19 period. That figure marked a significant deterioration compared to analysts' expectations for a 7% decline.

The economic malaise also weighed on the labor market. Unemployment inched lower to 5.1% in October, while the jobless rate for those aged between 16 and 24, excluding students, hovered near the highest level in a year, standing at 18% in September.

Growth in the world's second-largest economy bounced off three-year lows in the third quarter, expanding 4.5% from a year earlier. Goldman Sachs expects China's economy to slow to 4.6% this year, from 5% expansion in 2025, before easing further to 4.5% in 2027.

The Wall Street bank expects the low inflation to extend into next year, with consumer inflation ticking lower to 0.8% this year, 0.5 in 2027. It cited soft domestic demand, abundant aggregate supply, easing commodity-price momentum and potential artificial intelligence disruption risks.

Despite the moderation, the projected growth rate still puts the economy on track to achieve Beijing's ambition of doubling 2020 per capita income by 2035, Goldman Sachs said in a note Monday.

Subdued consumer inflation and roaring exports paint a picture of an economy increasingly dominant in global markets but struggling to generate prosperity at home. Consumer inflation ticked down to 0.7% in October, its first deceleration after two straight months of increases, while exports surged by a more-than-expected 35.2%.

China is on track to surpass last year's record $1.2 trillion trade surplus despite growing trade tensions, with the gap reaching the $1 trillion threshold by October — the fastest it has ever reached that mark.

The strong export momentum has largely been driven by global demand for AI-related technology and equipment. Any signs of pullback in the industry could weigh on China's exports, analysts warn.

"The market was already worried" about a slowdown in the AI capital-spending cycle even before the Nvidia scandal that put flows of restricted chips to China under renewed scrutiny, said Zhiwei Zhang, president and chief economist at Pinpoint Asset Management.

"Now the risk has become even more serious," Zhang said, forecasting further downside risk for global economic growth next year.

The increasingly lopsided growth came as major trading partners have ramped up calls for the economy to rebalance. Headwinds on China's export engine could intensify next year, with further trade restrictions from the European Union posing a critical downside risk, Citi Research analysts said.

"More EU trade measures targeting China look plausible to us, potentially adding to tensions ahead," the bank said, though Beijing is likely to be open to negotiating "reasonable, practical concessions," such as import pledges, sector-specific deals and value-added tax rebate cuts.

— CNBC's Evelyn Cheng contributed to this report.

#2775. A trading strategy to ride out the Nvidia uncertainty and generate income in the process

Jensen Huang's abrupt resignation has sent shockwaves through the tech sector, dragging Nvidia's (NVDA) stock down roughly 30% since Oct. 6. The market despises uncertainty, and losing a visionary founder, particularly one without a well-telegraphed succession plan, makes it worse.

However, for opportunistic investors, this panic has made the stock cheaper (obviously) while options have become significantly more expensive. Trading in the $160s, Nvidia's valuation has taken a big haircut, yet its underlying business fundamentals remain largely intact. While Huang was the architect of Nvidia's AI dominance, the company's moat — superior GPUs, the CUDA software ecosystem, and networking expertise — does not walk out the door with him. The company's structural advantage in the global artificial intelligence rollout persists.

Buying good companies at a discount is investing 101, but the current market dynamics may do something else for those who use options.  Huang's departure guarantees that Nvidia will be operating under a cloud of transition for the foreseeable future. Executing a permanent CEO search while navigating global regulatory scrutiny takes time. Consequently, Nvidia's implied volatility (IV)—the market's pricing of expected future price swings — has spiked to highs not seen since the tariff tantrum in the first half of 2025, and this elevated IV is highly likely to persist for several months as the leadership drama slowly unfolds.

This prolonged period of high implied volatility can be a goldmine for option sellers, specifically those executing options overlays such as covered call strategies. When you buy NVDA shares at these depressed levels and simultaneously sell out-of-the-money call options against your position, the premiums you collect are directly tied to IV. Because fear and transition-related uncertainty have dramatically inflated these option prices, the income generated from selling covered calls is now significantly higher than usual.

This lowers the risk profile of holding the stock considerably over time. Consistently collecting these inflated premiums systematically lowers your overall cost basis in the underlying shares. If the stock trades sideways or recovers slowly during the CEO search, you are generating an outsized cash yield on a stagnant asset. If the stock experiences further downside turbulence, the heavy premium income acts as a substantial downside buffer, meaningfully reducing the strategy's overall risk over time.

By deploying a persistent covered call strategy, investors can leverage the exact fear driving the stock down to generate robust, risk-reducing income while maintaining exposure to the undeniable future of AI.

#2773. Anthropic’s IPO lands as the embattled AI trade looks for a much-needed bright spot

Between rising interest rates, a nationwide AI backlash, a major political shift in Washington, D.C., and the sudden ouster of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang, the tech industry is under mounting strain.

Now comes the most important tech IPO in years, and a defining event for the burgeoning but still young artificial intelligence market.

With Anthropic expected to debut on the Nasdaq on Thursday, the stakes are sky-high for almost every major tech company. Advanced Micro Devices, Broadcom, Google, Micron, Microsoft, Nvidia and Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Company are collectively investing $26 billion at the IPO price, which is currently expected at $70 to $78 a share. Some of those companies are existing investors, as is early backer Amazon, whose stake could be in the hundreds of billions of dollars.

In addition to all the cash on the line, tech giants also need Anthropic's spending for many years to come. The Claude creator and its top rival, OpenAI, are driving an outsized amount of the AI buildout, and are burning historic amounts of money in the process.

Anthropic has outstanding spending commitments of over $900 billion, according to its latest prospectus. It's on the hook to spend at least $110 billion with Amazon over the course of a decade, and another $400 billion with other companies for cloud capacity, according to filings and partner disclosures. The lab also made $300 billion in commitments with data center and chip partners. Anthropic intends to lay out $125 billion to $150 billion for AI chips from Google, though delivery won't start until 2028.

"The numbers are just staggering," Joe Fath, a partner at venture firm Eclipse and former T. Rowe Price portfolio manager, said in an interview. "It's hard to get your head wrapped around it."

Cloud partners Amazon, Google and Microsoft account for 31% of contractual obligations, Anthropic said in its prospectus. Last year, Amazon built an Indiana data center just for Anthropic, using custom chips.

By going public now, Anthropic can raise a large portion of the money it needs to pay for the technology and infrastructure required for building and hosting its frontier models and expanding portfolio of services. The company recorded a net loss of $104 billion through the first nine months of 2026. The IPO could yield over $90 billion in proceeds, on top of Anthropic's $132 billion in existing cash, including short-term investments.

The length of Anthropic's contracts is also unique. Typically such contracts last five years or less, while Anthropic's agreement with Amazon extends for twice that amount of time.

"Realistically, anything past three to four years, you're basically throwing darts in the dark," said Corey Quinn, chief cloud economist at spend management software vendor Duckbill. "It is almost a certainty that it will be renegotiated long before the culmination of that contract."

Meanwhile, a data center for Anthropic that starts leasing in 2027 has a 20-year term. For both space and computing, Anthropic is splurging now so it doesn't end up short on capacity long into the future.

"In the past, we have significantly underestimated our compute requirements, which left us capacity constrained and impacted both product development and deployment," Anthropic said in the prospectus.

Anthropic and Amazon didn't provide a comment.

It's a tough time for Anthropic to hit the Nasdaq, with the offering coming as tech investors are desperate for some good news. The Nasdaq is down to start November after dropping 5.3% in October, its worst month since March 2025.

Rising interest rates pushed investors out of debt-heavy neoclouds, which have become a critical piece of the infrastructure buildout. And in the midterm elections earlier this month, Democrats won sweeping victories in both houses of Congress and in key gubernatorial races, while dozens of ballot measures were passed that will potentially limit new data center development across the country.

Then came the Nvidia crisis. Shares of the chipmaker at the center of the AI boom tumbled 13% this week to their lowest since July 2025, after Huang's multiple reported affairs, including one with an alleged Chinese spy, led to his ouster early Thursday. Huang was replaced on an interim basis with CFO Colette Cress, who faces a major test on Tuesday, two days before the Anthropic debut, as Nvidia is set to report quarterly results.

The ongoing Nvidia fallout and the Anthropic IPO create quite the split-screen moment for tech's heavyweights, which are closely tied to both companies.

Google will be paying particularly close attention.

The company is set to own about 13% of Anthropic after the IPO, and is backstopping the lab's $19 billion in lease commitments to Terawulf, which will supply data center space in Kentucky. If Anthropic goes insolvent, Google will step in and pay the remaining rent or a termination fee, Terawulf said.

"Everybody knows that Anthropic is going to be the future of Google," said Subhadeep Chatterjee, a former Google cloud partnerships leader who now advises companies.

One major impetus for Anthropic to enter costly and long-term guarantees is that OpenAI is being equally aggressive, and capacity is limited, particularly for graphics processing units. Going long can mean locking in rates as the industry confronts questions about whether data center development will slow.

"You will need to be in a rush to get infrastructure, and if you are not going to be there, you will be out of business," said Eli Mansoor, CEO of OskaQ Consulting, which advises companies on cloud negotiations. "It used to be that prices always go down, but this is changing, especially in the GPU world."

Big financial firms are lining up to help Anthropic get what it wants. Bank of America, Blackstone, Citigroup and Morgan Stanley have offered $63 billion to buy racks full of Broadcom AI chips that Anthropic will rent. Broadcom has provided a backstop with a maximum liability of $43 billion.

That's a lot of silicon and a massive bet for a five-year-old company that's never lived through a technology downturn.

"It's not a risk-free prospect, but I would call it smart investing," said David Tollen, founder of Sycamore Legal, where he specializes in IT contracts. "They're investing, in a sense, in themselves. They're putting money into something that if it succeeds, they get a piece of the action."

#2772. Cowboys’ Brian Schottenheimer Rips’Atrocious’ Defense

The Dallas Cowboys had one of the worst defensive games in the history of the franchise on Sunday against a high-powered 49ers attack, and while there were some encouraging signs with the way the team hung in and fought back against the league's best team, the fact that the defense yielded 584 yards of offense seemed to break coach Brian Schottenheimer's ability to level-headed and cool, as he usually is in press conferences, no matter the result. The Cowboys lost, 46-40, and that would not be such a bad result, except that the game was littered with poor tackling and enormous chunk plays by the 49ers that clearly raised Schottenheimer's blood pressure several points.

When he met with Cowboys reporters after the game, Schottenheimer was not shy about pointing to the disappointment he had in coordinator Christian Parker's defense.

Schottenheimer frequently takes blame himself when things go wrong for the Cowboys. Not on Sunday.

Here's what he said in full: "The second half was unacceptable. The tackling was atrocious. And we are going to do a deep dive on the film and see why. You can't give up three explosive plays of over 65 yards for touchdowns and expect to win in this league. Do a deep dive on the film but totally unacceptable defense in that second half."

He was asked about whether he thinks Parker can get the defense solved, and Schottenheimer did not give a wholeheartedly positive response: "We have to look at it. We are gonna watch a lot of film because there are a lot of things I have to figure out. I have never been around a situation like this where the defense was that poor in the second half. We rushed the passer well, got the five sacks …

"But what is killing us is missed tackles and explosive players. Go back to Indianapolis last week. We play good defense, give up a 65-yard touchdown. Play good defense, give up a 70-yard touchdown. Can't do that."

And yet, the Cowboys did. And the coach was not happy.

#2771. Observations after Brown-Maxey duo scores 58 points in Sixers' win without Embiid

Observations after Brown-Maxey duo scores 58 points in Sixers' win without Embiid originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Jaylen Brown-Tyrese Maxey duo starred, LeBron James reached another milestone, and the Sixers earned a win without Joel Embiid on Sunday afternoon.

They improved to 11-3 on the season with a 131-124 victory over the Pacers at Xfinity Mobile Arena.

Brown scored 31 points and Maxey posted 27.

LeBron James had 15 points and 11 assists. He passed Jason Kidd on Sunday for third in NBA history with 12,092 assists.

Pascal Siakam scored 31 points for the 5-9 Pacers.

On the first day of a back-to-back, the Sixers were down Embiid (right foot plantar fascia injury management), Dominick Barlow (lower back stress reaction), Adem Bona (cervical spine sprain) and Ariel Hukporti (right Achilles tendon tear). The Pacers' injured players included Tyrese Haliburton and Andrew Nembhard.

The Sixers will face the Nets on Monday night in Brooklyn. Here are observations on their win over the Pacers:

Jabari Walker started for the first time this season, opening the game at center. He's been in the Sixers' rotation the past five games since Bona's injury.

Walker set up the Sixers' first basket, swiping a steal and then tossing the ball ahead to Maxey for a fast-break dunk. Maxey and Brown both began well and combined to score all of the Sixers' first 15 points.

Though he conceded five inches to 7-foot Pacers center Ivica Zubac, Walker was solid in his first stint. He got an early post stop on Zubac and then snatched a strong defensive rebound.

Dean Wade was next up for the Sixers at the five. He sunk a three-pointer on his first shot to give the team a 20-16 lead.

Walker ended up playing 25 minutes and posting 11 points and seven rebounds. Going back to the preseason, his stock has steadily risen.

"Here we are again after another game saying Jabari played very well," Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said. "He plays hard, he rebounds, he's got another level of confidence with his shooting this year.

"It's kind of wash, rinse and repeat again with him (today). He was great. … Jabari went in there and battled (Zubac). I thought he certainly matched him, if not outplayed him, which is a heck of a job."

Wade had 12 points and seven boards in 35 minutes. Overall, the Sixers' lack of healthy conventional centers is still a clear deficiency. Of course, everything should be simpler once Embiid is back in action.

The Sixers' defense did not have a sparkling start. The Pacers tallied 35 points in the first quarter and shot 62.5 percent from the field.

Brown helped the Sixers avoid a substantial deficit by draining all sorts of jumpers. He rarely appears bothered by close contests. Brown moved to 7 for 9 from the floor when he knocked down a three early in the second quarter. Kentavious Caldwell-Pope's triple knotted the contest up at 37-all.

Nurse needed to stray from his Plan A rotation when VJ Edgecombe picked up his third foul with 9:56 left in the second quarter. Labaron Philon Jr., who got a game with the G League's Delaware Blue Coats on Friday night, subbed in.

For a rookie guard, Philon has looked comfortable and confident on offense in most of his NBA appearances. He swished a long-range jumper in the second quarter and saw a floater rim out.

Brown reached the 20-point mark and put the Sixers up one with a slam assisted by James late in the second quarter.

He ran into serious foul trouble in the third quarter. Brown was whistled for his fourth foul with 9:53 left in the period for an off-arm shove on Aaron Nesmith. Wade entered and Brown exited.

The Sixers' slim lead didn't slip away. Walker nailed threes on consecutive possessions and Maxey jammed in a dunk.

Soon after, Tacko Fall came in for his first meaningful minutes as a Sixer. The 7-foot-6 two-way contract player grabbed an offensive board off a missed Edgecombe free throw, which drew one of the day's loudest cheers. With Fall on the floor, Maxey and James connected on an alley-oop that extended the Sixers' lead to 82-73.

When the Sixers tried to sit in a zone, Fall was called for a defensive three-second violation. Whatever the scheme, most of Fall's defensive work will come around the paint. He certainly has exceptional physical traits as a rim protector and is not nimble on the perimeter.

Fall logged five scoreless third-quarter minutes and recorded three rebounds. The Sixers outscored Indiana by a point with Fall on the court. Given Fall is occupying a (two-way) roster slot and the Sixers are so depleted at center, it made sense to see a bit of what he can do outside of garbage time. While five minutes isn't enough for any grand conclusions, it's very safe to say Fall is a unique player.

"I'm 7-6, so I can change the game in so many ways," Fall said. "Offensively, setting screens, making sure guys get open, being an easy target for lobs. Defensively, I feel like I've always been an elite rim protector and I've worked on my body a lot to be able to move a lot better."

Nurse praised Fall's outing, saying he "gave us some great minutes and a nice lift."

Fall is appreciative of his first NBA opportunity since the 2021-22 season.

"I've worked very hard," he said. "God has brought me back here and I'm thankful. And I'm ready. Whenever my name is called, I'm going to go out there and do my job."

The Sixers built their advantage to 102-87 with a Brown three early in the fourth quarter. They couldn't cruise to a blowout, though.

Siakam kept hurting the Sixers as a driver and had a highly efficient fourth quarter, scoring 19 points on 7-for-9 shooting. Quenton Jackson's layup trimmed Indiana's deficit to 119-115.

The Sixers ultimately held off the Pacers' push.

Caldwell-Pope canned a three, Wade threw down an and-one dunk, and James checked off his milestone assist with a late dish to Edgecombe for a layup.

#2770. China confirms Xi-Trump-Putin historic summit. What will be on the agenda?

China confirmed Monday that President Xi Jinping will meet with Russian President Vladimir Putin and U.S. President Donald Trump in Shenzhen on Thursday, setting up the first-ever gathering of the three leaders.

That confirmation from a spokesperson at China's Foreign Ministry follows announcements from Moscow and the White House. The Wall Street Journal had reported last month that Putin had proposed a three-way sit-down in an attempt to negotiate an end to the four-year war in Ukraine.

While the summit among the three nations, on the sidelines of the APEC meeting in Shenzhen, is unprecedented, the leaders have held bilateral meetings over the years. Xi has met with Trump three times since October last year and more than 40 times with Putin since becoming president in 2013. Trump and Putin have met in person on several occasions, including a visit to Alaska by the Russian leader in August 2025.

"For Xi, just the ability to bring Putin and Trump to the table, a first among equals scenario, will deliver much of the political benefit even before the discussions begin," said Peter Alexander, Shanghai-based managing director at Z-Ben Advisors.

Trump has departed Washington, D.C. for a four-day trip to Asia that also includes a stop in the Philippines.

The main hurdle to the first-of-its-kind summit now appears to be "a disagreement over the agenda," said Neo Wang, chief China strategist at Evercore ISI.

The White House signaled the U.S. intention to prioritize talks over nuclear non-proliferation and the Middle East war, while downplaying the prospects of engaging in talks over Ukraine — a topic high on Moscow's agenda.

"He [Trump] will discuss the reduction of nuclear weapons in the world, whose proliferation President Trump believes is very dangerous," Olivia Wales, a White House spokeswoman, told reporters on a briefing call. The president will raise the U.S.-Israeli war against Iran in the meeting but is not expected to bring up Russia's war in Ukraine, the official said.

Putin's position on the war appears unwavering, with the Russian president telling U.S. envoys Jared Kushner and Steve Witkoff in a meeting in Moscow last week that Europe should "figure out" whether "they want peace or war." The meeting came a day after an earlier round of talks aimed at ending the war in Ukraine wrapped up in Abu Dhabi.

Russian presidential aide Yuri Ushakov said last week the talks in Shenzhen would continue the diplomatic efforts aimed at reaching a negotiated settlement to the Ukraine war, according to state-controlled local media Russia Today.

China and Russia could turn more aggressive with their military ambitions over the next two years if they perceive Trump as politically weakened, Brookings Institution senior fellow Robert Kagan told CNBC earlier this month, following the dramatic defeat of Trump's Republican Party in the midterm election.

China did not specify what will be discussed at the three leaders' meeting in Monday's statement.

"Xi may be inclined to do Putin a favor by showing up, given close strategic ties," said Wang. But a joint statement on Ukraine territory, security arrangements or sanctions against Russia appears unlikely, he said, as it would leave Beijing "more exposed to political scrutiny and accountability."

A U.S.-China trade truce set to expire in January is likely to be on the table, with analysts largely expecting an extension despite signs of growing friction in the bilateral relationship.

Aside from an immediate extension, leaders may also look to assess "whether a more durable framework for a rebalancing of U.S.–China trade relations can be established," said Carlos Casanova, senior economist for Asia at UBP.

U.S. trade deficit with China remains substantial, with Beijing's exports to Washington recording double-digit growth for most of this year, from last year's low base. Narrowing that deficit will be central to Washington's call for a more balanced trade, Casanova said.

#2769. Steelers OL Mason McCormick carted off field vs. Bengals

Down 17-0 in the first half versus the rival Cincinnati Bengals on Sunday Night Football, the Steelers' rough night continued with starting left guard Mason McCormick being carted off the field.

With 1:27 remaining in the half, McCormick was seen lying on the field with his right ankle at an uncomfortable angle.

His leg was placed in an air cast according to the Pittsburgh Post-Gazette's Ray Fittipaldo, and McCormick was carted off the field. The play was a fourth down, with Steelers kicker Chris Boswell bringing the score to 17-3 on a 45-yard field goal on the following play.

Spencer Anderson is expected to replace McCormick in the starting lineup.

After Boswell's field goal, the Bengals again drove down the field to bring the first half score to 24-7 in Cincinnati's favor. A battle between the two top teams in the AFC North has quickly turned sour for Pittsburgh, which is now without one of its top players on offense.
